========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… what finally made me come in is that I don’t feel like myself in the way I used to. The part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of my work, or yoga, or going to galleries—things I used to really like—has just gone very quiet.

Even journaling, which I used to do all the time, feels like… I don’t know, like moving a pen around because I’m supposed to. Mostly I’m just getting through obligations. Work, errands, answering people when I have to. It’s strange because I’m a social worker, so I know how this probably sounds, and I keep thinking about clients who describe feeling flattened, though I don’t want to make it about work. But yes, that’s what brought me in.

I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.

It’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.

I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, I misplace my train of thought sometimes, but I also feel like everyone does that, especially when there’s too many little things going on.

At work I’ll be reading something and then remember I need to get paper towels at home, and then I’m thinking about the kitchen cabinet that doesn’t close right—so it’s not always that I can’t focus, it’s more like my mind wanders off. Decisions… I don’t know. I still make them, I think. Maybe I take longer with small things, like what to cook, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change in thinking clearly.

The part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of my work, or yoga, or going to galleries—things I used to really like—has just gone very quiet.\n\nEven journaling, which I used to do all the time, feels like… I don’t know, like moving a pen around because I’m supposed to. Mostly I’m just getting through obligations. Work, errands, answering people when I have to. It’s strange because I’m a social worker, so I know how this probably sounds, and I keep thinking about clients who describe feeling flattened, though I don’t want to make it about work. But yes, that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest or enjoyment in activities like work, yoga, or going to galleries—things you used to really like?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like those things still exist on my calendar, or in my apartment—my yoga mat is right there, my journal is on the table—but the part of me that used to actually want them feels very far away.\n\nWork especially is strange, because I can still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel much from it. And galleries, journaling… I used to get pulled into those things. Now it’s more like I’m just moving through obligations, checking boxes. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired, but it feels quieter than that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, though I keep wondering if I’m just pinning it on that because it was such a clear change in the household.\n\nBut yes… this whole way I’ve been thinking and working and relating to people, or not really connecting the same way, has been going on since then. Around eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless exactly. Down, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from being kind of worn thin, if that makes sense.\n\nI know you asked “most of the day” and “two weeks,” and I’m trying to answer that cleanly, but my mind goes to how the days kind of blur when I’m just going to work and coming home and doing the household things. Like, I’ll think, “Was Tuesday bad, or was that just the day the sink was making that noise?” So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear stretch like that. It’s more like a dullness than feeling really sad all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way you’re asking. I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I misplace my train of thought sometimes, but I also feel like everyone does that, especially when there’s too many little things going on.\n\nAt work I’ll be reading something and then remember I need to get paper towels at home, and then I’m thinking about the kitchen cabinet that doesn’t close right—so it’s not always that I can’t focus, it’s more like my mind wanders off. Decisions… I don’t know. I still make them, I think. Maybe I take longer with small things, like what to cook, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change in thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up a lot during the night, or sleeping much more than usual—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track of it that closely. I mean, some nights I stay up later than I meant to, but that can happen because I start doing one little thing and then I’m wiping down the counter or checking whether I paid a bill, and then somehow it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve had trouble sleeping nearly every day or anything like that. I’m not waking up thinking, “Oh, this has been going on for weeks.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

And galleries, journaling… I used to get pulled into those things. Now it’s more like I’m just moving through obligations, checking boxes. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired, but it feels quieter than that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, though I keep wondering if I’m just pinning it on that because it was such a clear change in the household.\n\nBut yes… this whole way I’ve been thinking and working and relating to people, or not really connecting the same way, has been going on since then. Around eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless exactly. Down, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from being kind of worn thin, if that makes sense.\n\nI know you asked “most of the day” and “two weeks,” and I’m trying to answer that cleanly, but my mind goes to how the days kind of blur when I’m just going to work and coming home and doing the household things. Like, I’ll think, “Was Tuesday bad, or was that just the day the sink was making that noise?” So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear stretch like that. It’s more like a dullness than feeling really sad all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way you’re asking. I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’m not waking up thinking, “Oh, this has been going on for weeks.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my appetite kind of depends on the day and what’s around—if there’s leftovers I’ll eat that, or sometimes I realize I forgot to buy actual food and then I’m eating toast or something.\n\nBut I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone saying I look like I lost weight. I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at home is under the sink, and honestly that cabinet is a mess… I keep meaning to clear it out.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed eating much less nearly every day or losing weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I’m kind of… blah, or I get irritated and then I start thinking maybe I should reorganize the pantry or finally deal with that pile of mail by the door, but I wouldn’t call it sad most of the day.\n\nTwo weeks in a row… I don’t know, I haven’t been marking it down or anything. Around eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless exactly. Down, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from being kind of worn thin, if that makes sense.\n\nI know you asked “most of the day” and “two weeks,” and I’m trying to answer that cleanly, but my mind goes to how the days kind of blur when I’m just going to work and coming home and doing the household things. Like, I’ll think, “Was Tuesday bad, or was that just the day the sink was making that noise?” So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear stretch like that. It’s more like a dullness than feeling really sad all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way you’re asking. I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is that I don’t feel like myself in the way I used to. It’s like those things still exist on my calendar, or in my apartment—my yoga mat is right there, my journal is on the table—but the part of me that used to actually want them feels very far away.\n\nWork especially is strange, because I can still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel much from it. Down, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from being kind of worn thin, if that makes sense.\n\nI know you asked “most of the day” and “two weeks,” and I’m trying to answer that cleanly, but my mind goes to how the days kind of blur when I’m just going to work and coming home and doing the household things. It’s more like a dullness than feeling really sad all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way you’re asking. I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at home is under the sink, and honestly that cabinet is a mess… I keep meaning to clear it out.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed eating much less nearly every day or losing weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Yeah… that’s more like it, actually.

It’s not that I hate those things now. I’ll still go through the motions sometimes—work, yoga, maybe thinking I should go see an exhibit because I used to love that kind of thing. But the part of me that used to reach for it, like really look forward to it or feel fed by it, has gotten very quiet.

Yes… I think that’s the thing. Maybe not every single minute, but for a stretch, yeah. I’m not really participating the way I used to.

It’s like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of work, yoga, galleries, even just sitting with my journal—that part has gone very quiet. I still move through things because they’re on the list, you know? Like obligations. I’ll think, “Oh, I should go to yoga,” and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer or checking the same email twice, and the day is gone.

It’s like those things still exist on my calendar, or in my apartment—my yoga mat is right there, my journal is on the table—but the part of me that used to actually want them feels very far away.\n\nWork especially is strange, because I can still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel much from it. I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired, but it feels quieter than that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, though I keep wondering if I’m just pinning it on that because it was such a clear change in the household.\n\nBut yes… this whole way I’ve been thinking and working and relating to people, or not really connecting the same way, has been going on since then. Around eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless exactly. Down, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from being kind of worn thin, if that makes sense.\n\nI know you asked “most of the day” and “two weeks,” and I’m trying to answer that cleanly, but my mind goes to how the days kind of blur when I’m just going to work and coming home and doing the household things. Like, I’ll think, “Was Tuesday bad, or was that just the day the sink was making that noise?” So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear stretch like that. It’s more like a dullness than feeling really sad all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way you’re asking. I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I misplace my train of thought sometimes, but I also feel like everyone does that, especially when there’s too many little things going on.\n\nAt work I’ll be reading something and then remember I need to get paper towels at home, and then I’m thinking about the kitchen cabinet that doesn’t close right—so it’s not always that I can’t focus, it’s more like my mind wanders off. Decisions… I don’t know. I still make them, I think. Maybe I take longer with small things, like what to cook, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change in thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up a lot during the night, or sleeping much more than usual—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track of it that closely. I mean, some nights I stay up later than I meant to, but that can happen because I start doing one little thing and then I’m wiping down the counter or checking whether I paid a bill, and then somehow it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve had trouble sleeping nearly every day or anything like that. I’m not waking up thinking, “Oh, this has been going on for weeks.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my appetite kind of depends on the day and what’s around—if there’s leftovers I’ll eat that, or sometimes I realize I forgot to buy actual food and then I’m eating toast or something.\n\nBut I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone saying I look like I lost weight. I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at home is under the sink, and honestly that cabinet is a mess… I keep meaning to clear it out.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed eating much less nearly every day or losing weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I’m kind of… blah, or I get irritated and then I start thinking maybe I should reorganize the pantry or finally deal with that pile of mail by the door, but I wouldn’t call it sad most of the day.\n\nTwo weeks in a row… I don’t know, I haven’t been marking it down or anything. But no, I haven’t noticed being down nearly every day like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not like that. I mean, I already kind of said that, right? I get off days, sure—who doesn’t—but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day every day for two weeks.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel low and then get distracted by something else, like I’ll start sorting papers and then I find an old receipt and wonder why I kept it, and suddenly I’m doing that instead. But I wouldn’t say it’s been this constant sadness nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Right, I know you asked that already—sorry. I guess my answer is still no, or at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI have little dips, like I’ll feel off and then end up fussing with something in the house, like the mail or the kitchen drawer with all the takeout menus—actually I don’t know why I keep those anymore—but it doesn’t stay as “sad most of the day” for weeks. Not nearly every day like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like yoga or going to galleries, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s more like it, actually.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. I’ll still go through the motions sometimes—work, yoga, maybe thinking I should go see an exhibit because I used to love that kind of thing. But the part of me that used to reach for it, like really look forward to it or feel fed by it, has gotten very quiet.\n\nEven journaling, which used to help me sort myself out—I’ll open the notebook and then just sit there, or write a sentence and feel like, what’s the point. And at work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, because people are counting on me, but it feels more like obligations than something that gives me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood, such as feeling sad, empty, or hopeless, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in that steady, most-of-the-day kind of way you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel empty for a bit, like I’m just sitting there and the room feels too quiet, and then I notice the plant needs water or I remember I forgot to return a library book from ages ago—actually I don’t know if I ever found that book—but no, I don’t think it’s been nearly every day for two weeks where I felt sad or hopeless most of the day. At least not that I can clearly say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a marked decrease in your interest in activities like yoga, galleries, or journaling—so much so that you weren’t really participating in them—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the thing. Maybe not every single minute, but for a stretch, yeah. I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nIt’s like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of work, yoga, galleries, even just sitting with my journal—that part has gone very quiet. I still move through things because they’re on the list, you know? Like obligations. I’ll think, “Oh, I should go to yoga,” and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer or checking the same email twice, and the day is gone.\n\nSo yes, that decrease in interest… that’s really what finally made me come in. Not sadness exactly, more like everything that used to pull me forward doesn’t pull anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And at work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, because people are counting on me, but it feels more like obligations than something that gives me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood, such as feeling sad, empty, or hopeless, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in that steady, most-of-the-day kind of way you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel empty for a bit, like I’m just sitting there and the room feels too quiet, and then I notice the plant needs water or I remember I forgot to return a library book from ages ago—actually I don’t know if I ever found that book—but no, I don’t think it’s been nearly every day for two weeks where I felt sad or hopeless most of the day. At least not that I can clearly say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a marked decrease in your interest in activities like yoga, galleries, or journaling—so much so that you weren’t really participating in them—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the thing. Maybe not every single minute, but for a stretch, yeah. I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nIt’s like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of work, yoga, galleries, even just sitting with my journal—that part has gone very quiet. I still move through things because they’re on the list, you know? Like obligations. I’ll think, “Oh, I should go to yoga,” and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer or checking the same email twice, and the day is gone.\n\nSo yes, that decrease in interest… that’s really what finally made me come in. Not sadness exactly, more like everything that used to pull me forward doesn’t pull anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I know I’ve felt kind of empty sometimes, like there’s a blankness to things, but “sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day”… I don’t know.

It’s hard because days blur together a bit, and then I start thinking about whether I’m using the words right. Like, if I’m sitting on the couch for an hour and not really wanting to do anything, is that sad? Or is that just… I don’t know, being tired of the day? And then I remember something practical, like the laundry, or I see a message I meant to answer, and I get pulled into that.

So I can’t really say yes to the two-weeks, most-of-the-day thing. I haven’t noticed it that clearly.

Around eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless exactly. Down, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from being kind of worn thin, if that makes sense.\n\nI know you asked “most of the day” and “two weeks,” and I’m trying to answer that cleanly, but my mind goes to how the days kind of blur when I’m just going to work and coming home and doing the household things. Like, I’ll think, “Was Tuesday bad, or was that just the day the sink was making that noise?” So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear stretch like that. It’s more like a dullness than feeling really sad all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that clear way you’re asking. I have off days, but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day nearly every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of flat or distracted, and then I’ll start thinking about everything else—laundry, work emails, whether I forgot something at the store—and I lose track of what I’m even feeling. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of persistent sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt unusually fatigued for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s that kind of tired you mean. Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I misplace my train of thought sometimes, but I also feel like everyone does that, especially when there’s too many little things going on.\n\nAt work I’ll be reading something and then remember I need to get paper towels at home, and then I’m thinking about the kitchen cabinet that doesn’t close right—so it’s not always that I can’t focus, it’s more like my mind wanders off. Decisions… I don’t know. I still make them, I think. Maybe I take longer with small things, like what to cook, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change in thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up a lot during the night, or sleeping much more than usual—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track of it that closely. I mean, some nights I stay up later than I meant to, but that can happen because I start doing one little thing and then I’m wiping down the counter or checking whether I paid a bill, and then somehow it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve had trouble sleeping nearly every day or anything like that. I’m not waking up thinking, “Oh, this has been going on for weeks.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my appetite kind of depends on the day and what’s around—if there’s leftovers I’ll eat that, or sometimes I realize I forgot to buy actual food and then I’m eating toast or something.\n\nBut I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone saying I look like I lost weight. I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at home is under the sink, and honestly that cabinet is a mess… I keep meaning to clear it out.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed eating much less nearly every day or losing weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. But no, I haven’t noticed being down nearly every day like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not like that. I mean, I already kind of said that, right? I get off days, sure—who doesn’t—but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day every day for two weeks.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel low and then get distracted by something else, like I’ll start sorting papers and then I find an old receipt and wonder why I kept it, and suddenly I’m doing that instead. But I wouldn’t say it’s been this constant sadness nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Right, I know you asked that already—sorry. I guess my answer is still no, or at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI have little dips, like I’ll feel off and then end up fussing with something in the house, like the mail or the kitchen drawer with all the takeout menus—actually I don’t know why I keep those anymore—but it doesn’t stay as “sad most of the day” for weeks. Across the whole eight months? I’m honestly not sure. I know there have been patches where I felt kind of blank or muted, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” I start trying to line it up like a calendar, and I can’t really do that.

I mean, I misplace my train of thought sometimes, but I also feel like everyone does that, especially when there’s too many little things going on.\n\nAt work I’ll be reading something and then remember I need to get paper towels at home, and then I’m thinking about the kitchen cabinet that doesn’t close right—so it’s not always that I can’t focus, it’s more like my mind wanders off. Decisions… I don’t know. I still make them, I think. Maybe I take longer with small things, like what to cook, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some big change in thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up a lot during the night, or sleeping much more than usual—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track of it that closely. I mean, some nights I stay up later than I meant to, but that can happen because I start doing one little thing and then I’m wiping down the counter or checking whether I paid a bill, and then somehow it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve had trouble sleeping nearly every day or anything like that. I’m not waking up thinking, “Oh, this has been going on for weeks.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my appetite kind of depends on the day and what’s around—if there’s leftovers I’ll eat that, or sometimes I realize I forgot to buy actual food and then I’m eating toast or something.\n\nBut I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone saying I look like I lost weight. I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at home is under the sink, and honestly that cabinet is a mess… I keep meaning to clear it out.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed eating much less nearly every day or losing weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I’m kind of… blah, or I get irritated and then I start thinking maybe I should reorganize the pantry or finally deal with that pile of mail by the door, but I wouldn’t call it sad most of the day.\n\nTwo weeks in a row… I don’t know, I haven’t been marking it down or anything. But no, I haven’t noticed being down nearly every day like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not like that. I mean, I already kind of said that, right? I get off days, sure—who doesn’t—but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day every day for two weeks.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel low and then get distracted by something else, like I’ll start sorting papers and then I find an old receipt and wonder why I kept it, and suddenly I’m doing that instead. But I wouldn’t say it’s been this constant sadness nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Right, I know you asked that already—sorry. I guess my answer is still no, or at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI have little dips, like I’ll feel off and then end up fussing with something in the house, like the mail or the kitchen drawer with all the takeout menus—actually I don’t know why I keep those anymore—but it doesn’t stay as “sad most of the day” for weeks. Not nearly every day like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like yoga or going to galleries, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s more like it, actually.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. I’ll still go through the motions sometimes—work, yoga, maybe thinking I should go see an exhibit because I used to love that kind of thing. But the part of me that used to reach for it, like really look forward to it or feel fed by it, has gotten very quiet.\n\nEven journaling, which used to help me sort myself out—I’ll open the notebook and then just sit there, or write a sentence and feel like, what’s the point. And at work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, because people are counting on me, but it feels more like obligations than something that gives me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood, such as feeling sad, empty, or hopeless, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in that steady, most-of-the-day kind of way you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel empty for a bit, like I’m just sitting there and the room feels too quiet, and then I notice the plant needs water or I remember I forgot to return a library book from ages ago—actually I don’t know if I ever found that book—but no, I don’t think it’s been nearly every day for two weeks where I felt sad or hopeless most of the day. At least not that I can clearly say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a marked decrease in your interest in activities like yoga, galleries, or journaling—so much so that you weren’t really participating in them—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the thing. Maybe not every single minute, but for a stretch, yeah. I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nIt’s like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of work, yoga, galleries, even just sitting with my journal—that part has gone very quiet. I still move through things because they’re on the list, you know? Like obligations. I’ll think, “Oh, I should go to yoga,” and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer or checking the same email twice, and the day is gone.\n\nSo yes, that decrease in interest… that’s really what finally made me come in. Not sadness exactly, more like everything that used to pull me forward doesn’t pull anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I know I’ve felt kind of empty sometimes, like there’s a blankness to things, but “sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day”… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s hard because days blur together a bit, and then I start thinking about whether I’m using the words right. Like, if I’m sitting on the couch for an hour and not really wanting to do anything, is that sad? Or is that just… I don’t know, being tired of the day? And then I remember something practical, like the laundry, or I see a message I meant to answer, and I get pulled into that.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes to the two-weeks, most-of-the-day thing. I haven’t noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m repeating myself, sorry—my brain keeps circling it. I don’t really have a clean yes to that.\n\nThere are moments where I feel empty, or like things are kind of flat, but I can’t honestly say it’s been most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks. I’d notice a plant leaning toward the window, or I’d think about a client note template from work—well, not work exactly, just the way forms have boxes for feelings and life doesn’t—and then I’m off thinking about something else.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that in the way you’re describing. Not that consistently.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row at any time during the last eight months?\n\nPatient: Across the whole eight months? I’m honestly not sure. I know there have been patches where I felt kind of blank or muted, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” I start trying to line it up like a calendar, and I can’t really do that.\n\nI remember little pieces more than stretches—like standing in the kitchen with the kettle on and forgetting why I came in, or looking at my journal and feeling like it belonged to someone else. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, I keep trying to be careful because “empty” is a word I might use for a few hours or a day, but not necessarily like… a whole two-week block.

When I look back over the last eight months, it’s sort of scattered in my mind. I can remember ordinary things more clearly than moods sometimes—like the way the light came through the blinds, or whether I had enough coffee, or some form I needed to fill out—and then I lose the thread of what I was supposed to be tracking.

Some days I feel like I’ve been carrying groceries up stairs all day even if I haven’t, but then other days I’m sort of just moving through things.\n\nIt’s hard to say “nearly every day for two weeks,” because I don’t track it that neatly. I’ll think I’m tired, and then I remember the laundry’s been sitting in the machine too long, or someone at work asked me for something, and then I’m just on to the next thing. So… I haven’t really noticed a definite stretch like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, some nights I stay up later than I meant to, but that can happen because I start doing one little thing and then I’m wiping down the counter or checking whether I paid a bill, and then somehow it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’ve had trouble sleeping nearly every day or anything like that. I’m not waking up thinking, “Oh, this has been going on for weeks.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight—like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying—nearly every day during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my appetite kind of depends on the day and what’s around—if there’s leftovers I’ll eat that, or sometimes I realize I forgot to buy actual food and then I’m eating toast or something.\n\nBut I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone saying I look like I lost weight. I don’t weigh myself much. The scale at home is under the sink, and honestly that cabinet is a mess… I keep meaning to clear it out.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed eating much less nearly every day or losing weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I’m kind of… blah, or I get irritated and then I start thinking maybe I should reorganize the pantry or finally deal with that pile of mail by the door, but I wouldn’t call it sad most of the day.\n\nTwo weeks in a row… I don’t know, I haven’t been marking it down or anything. But no, I haven’t noticed being down nearly every day like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not like that. I mean, I already kind of said that, right? I get off days, sure—who doesn’t—but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day every day for two weeks.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel low and then get distracted by something else, like I’ll start sorting papers and then I find an old receipt and wonder why I kept it, and suddenly I’m doing that instead. But I wouldn’t say it’s been this constant sadness nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Right, I know you asked that already—sorry. Not nearly every day like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like yoga or going to galleries, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s more like it, actually.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. I’ll still go through the motions sometimes—work, yoga, maybe thinking I should go see an exhibit because I used to love that kind of thing. And at work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, because people are counting on me, but it feels more like obligations than something that gives me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood, such as feeling sad, empty, or hopeless, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in that steady, most-of-the-day kind of way you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel empty for a bit, like I’m just sitting there and the room feels too quiet, and then I notice the plant needs water or I remember I forgot to return a library book from ages ago—actually I don’t know if I ever found that book—but no, I don’t think it’s been nearly every day for two weeks where I felt sad or hopeless most of the day. At least not that I can clearly say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a marked decrease in your interest in activities like yoga, galleries, or journaling—so much so that you weren’t really participating in them—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the thing. But no, I haven’t noticed being down nearly every day like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not like that. I mean, I already kind of said that, right? I get off days, sure—who doesn’t—but I don’t remember a stretch where I was sad most of the day every day for two weeks.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel low and then get distracted by something else, like I’ll start sorting papers and then I find an old receipt and wonder why I kept it, and suddenly I’m doing that instead. But I wouldn’t say it’s been this constant sadness nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Right, I know you asked that already—sorry. I guess my answer is still no, or at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI have little dips, like I’ll feel off and then end up fussing with something in the house, like the mail or the kitchen drawer with all the takeout menus—actually I don’t know why I keep those anymore—but it doesn’t stay as “sad most of the day” for weeks. Not nearly every day like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like yoga or going to galleries, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s more like it, actually.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. I’ll still go through the motions sometimes—work, yoga, maybe thinking I should go see an exhibit because I used to love that kind of thing. But the part of me that used to reach for it, like really look forward to it or feel fed by it, has gotten very quiet.\n\nEven journaling, which used to help me sort myself out—I’ll open the notebook and then just sit there, or write a sentence and feel like, what’s the point. And at work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, because people are counting on me, but it feels more like obligations than something that gives me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood, such as feeling sad, empty, or hopeless, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in that steady, most-of-the-day kind of way you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel empty for a bit, like I’m just sitting there and the room feels too quiet, and then I notice the plant needs water or I remember I forgot to return a library book from ages ago—actually I don’t know if I ever found that book—but no, I don’t think it’s been nearly every day for two weeks where I felt sad or hopeless most of the day. At least not that I can clearly say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a marked decrease in your interest in activities like yoga, galleries, or journaling—so much so that you weren’t really participating in them—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the thing. Maybe not every single minute, but for a stretch, yeah. I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nIt’s like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of work, yoga, galleries, even just sitting with my journal—that part has gone very quiet. I still move through things because they’re on the list, you know? Like obligations. I’ll think, “Oh, I should go to yoga,” and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer or checking the same email twice, and the day is gone.\n\nSo yes, that decrease in interest… that’s really what finally made me come in. Two years… I don’t think I can really say yes to that. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt kind of empty or worn down, but “most of the day, more days than not, for two years” sounds very specific, and I don’t have that kind of clear pattern in my head.

I’ll still go through the motions sometimes—work, yoga, maybe thinking I should go see an exhibit because I used to love that kind of thing. But the part of me that used to reach for it, like really look forward to it or feel fed by it, has gotten very quiet.\n\nEven journaling, which used to help me sort myself out—I’ll open the notebook and then just sit there, or write a sentence and feel like, what’s the point. And at work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, because people are counting on me, but it feels more like obligations than something that gives me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood, such as feeling sad, empty, or hopeless, for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in that steady, most-of-the-day kind of way you mean.\n\nI mean, I can feel empty for a bit, like I’m just sitting there and the room feels too quiet, and then I notice the plant needs water or I remember I forgot to return a library book from ages ago—actually I don’t know if I ever found that book—but no, I don’t think it’s been nearly every day for two weeks where I felt sad or hopeless most of the day. At least not that I can clearly say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a marked decrease in your interest in activities like yoga, galleries, or journaling—so much so that you weren’t really participating in them—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the thing. Maybe not every single minute, but for a stretch, yeah. I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nIt’s like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something out of work, yoga, galleries, even just sitting with my journal—that part has gone very quiet. I still move through things because they’re on the list, you know? Like obligations. I’ll think, “Oh, I should go to yoga,” and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer or checking the same email twice, and the day is gone.\n\nSo yes, that decrease in interest… that’s really what finally made me come in. Not sadness exactly, more like everything that used to pull me forward doesn’t pull anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I know I’ve felt kind of empty sometimes, like there’s a blankness to things, but “sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day”… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s hard because days blur together a bit, and then I start thinking about whether I’m using the words right. Like, if I’m sitting on the couch for an hour and not really wanting to do anything, is that sad? Or is that just… I don’t know, being tired of the day? And then I remember something practical, like the laundry, or I see a message I meant to answer, and I get pulled into that.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes to the two-weeks, most-of-the-day thing. I haven’t noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row during this time?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m repeating myself, sorry—my brain keeps circling it. I don’t really have a clean yes to that.\n\nThere are moments where I feel empty, or like things are kind of flat, but I can’t honestly say it’s been most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks. I’d notice a plant leaning toward the window, or I’d think about a client note template from work—well, not work exactly, just the way forms have boxes for feelings and life doesn’t—and then I’m off thinking about something else.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that in the way you’re describing. Not that consistently.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row at any time during the last eight months?\n\nPatient: Across the whole eight months? I’m honestly not sure. I know there have been patches where I felt kind of blank or muted, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” I start trying to line it up like a calendar, and I can’t really do that.\n\nI remember little pieces more than stretches—like standing in the kitchen with the kettle on and forgetting why I came in, or looking at my journal and feeling like it belonged to someone else. But then another day I’d be distracted by errands or some article I was reading about museum lighting, of all things, and I wouldn’t have called myself sad exactly.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say yes. I haven’t noticed a clear two-week period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt sad, empty, or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row at any time during the last eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I keep trying to be careful because “empty” is a word I might use for a few hours or a day, but not necessarily like… a whole two-week block.\n\nWhen I look back over the last eight months, it’s sort of scattered in my mind. I can remember ordinary things more clearly than moods sometimes—like the way the light came through the blinds, or whether I had enough coffee, or some form I needed to fill out—and then I lose the thread of what I was supposed to be tracking.\n\nSo I haven’t noticed a stretch where I was sad or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks. Not in a way I can say yes to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt a depressed mood — like sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness — for most of the day, more days than not, over a period of at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years… I don’t think I can really say yes to that.
